An asymmetric version of Elekes-Szabó via group actions Thanks: Both authors were partially supported by DFG EXC 2044–390685587.
Martin Bays and Tingxiang Zou

Abstract
. We consider when finite families F ⊆ ℂ ⁡ [ t ] F\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}[t] of bounded degree polynomials, or more generally of bounded complexity finite-to-finite correspondences on ℂ {\mathbb{C}} , can exhibit non-expansion of the form | F ⁡ ( A ) | = O ⁡ ( | A | 1 + η ) |F(A)|=O(|A|^{1+\eta}) in their actions on finite sets A ⊆ ℂ A\subseteq{\mathbb{C}} with | F | ≫ | A | ε ≫ 1 |F|\gg|A|^{\varepsilon}\gg 1 , for a fixed ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 and arbitrarily small η > 0 \eta>0 . Our conclusions generalise the Elekes-Rónyai and Elekes-Szabó theorems, which correspond to the case that F F is parametrised by a single complex variable and | F | = | A | |F|=|A| . Our result also applies to families of correspondences between varieties of arbitrary dimension if we impose a general position assumption on A A . In all cases, the conclusion is that a commutative algebraic group structure is responsible. As a special case, we obtain asymmetric versions of Elekes-Rónyai and Elekes-Szabó, with explicit bounds on exponents. Our methods originate in model theory.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 11B30, Secondary 03C98

1.
Introduction

A polynomial
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x,y]
in two variables is
additive
if it is of
the form
f(x,y)=g(h(x)+k(y))
for some
g,h,k\in{\mathbb{R}}[t]
, and

multiplicative
if it is of the form
g(h(x)\cdot k(y))
. The Elekes-Rónyai
Theorem states that any other polynomial is
expanding
, in the
following sense:

Fact 1.1

(Elekes-Rónyai
[
8
]
,
[
7
]
)
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x,y]
be neither additive nor multiplicative.
Then there exist
c,\eta>0
(depending only on
\deg(f)
) such that for all

A,B\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
with
|A|=|B|=n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

|f(A,B)|=|\{f(a,b):a\in A,\;b\in B\}|\geq cn^{1+\eta}.

Subsequently, explicit exponents
\eta
were obtained, with
\eta=\frac{1}{2}
the
current record
[
21
, Corollary 1.5]
, and it was observed (see
[
7
, § 1.2]
)
that
{\mathbb{R}}
can be replaced with
{\mathbb{C}}
(with
\eta=\frac{1}{3}
).

A related theorem of Elekes and Szabó treats the more general setting where
an arbitrary algebraic surface replaces the graph
z=f(x,y)
:

Fact 1.2

(Elekes-Szabó
[
9
]
)
.

There is
\eta>0
(
\eta=\frac{1}{6}
works) such that
if
S\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
is an irreducible complex algebraic surface which does
not project in any two co-ordinates to a curve,
and if for all
c>0
there are
A_{i}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
with
|A_{i}|=n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
|S\cap(A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3})|\geq cn^{2-\eta}
,
then
S\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
is in co-ordinatewise correspondence (see
Definition
8.2
) with the graph of the group operation of a
1-dimensional algebraic group.

A natural higher-dimensional generalisation would consider a subvariety
V\subseteq W_{1}\times W_{2}\times W_{3}
of a product of three complex varieties,
and look for conditions under which
V
admits asymptotically large
intersections with products
A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
of finite subsets
A_{i}\subseteq W_{i}
.
One version of this is achieved in
[
9
, Theorem 27]
, but only
under a strong
general position
assumption on the
A_{i}
(slightly
weakened in
[
1
]
).

The main result of this paper is a version which removes the general position
assumption in one co-ordinate, say for
A_{2}
, and also allows
|A_{2}|
to be
much smaller than
|A_{1}|=|A_{3}|
.
To motivate this, we first present a special case generalising the
Elekes-Rónyai theorem.

Given a polynomial
f(x,y)\in{\mathbb{C}}[x,y]
and finite sets
A,B\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
, we
can view
B
as defining a finite set
F:=\{f(x,b):b\in B\}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}[x]
of polynomials in one variable, so
f(A,B)=F(A):=\{f(a):f\in F,\;a\in A\}
. This suggests a generalisation: given a finite set of
polynomials
F\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}[x]
and a finite set
A\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
, when does

|F(A)|
not expand? From this point of view, a constraint
|F|=|A|
is
unnatural, and we are led to an asymmetric view of expansion as meaning

|F(A)|\geq|A|^{1+\eta}
.
In the case
F=\{f(x,b):b\in B\}
, the polynomials in
F
are of degree
bounded independently of
B
, and it turns out that this is all that is needed
to obtain expansion from polynomials which are neither additive nor
multiplicative.
More precisely, define
F
to be
\varepsilon
-additive

if there are
g,h\in{\mathbb{C}}[t]
such that

|F\cap\{g(h(x)+a):a\in{\mathbb{C}}\}|\geq|F|^{1-\varepsilon}
,
and define
\varepsilon
-multiplicative
similarly.
Observe that if
f(x,y)
is additive, then
F=\{f(x,b):b\in B\}

is 0-additive.
We show:

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
9.4
)
.

For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
c,\eta>0

such that
for any finite set
F\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}[x]
of
non-constant polynomials of degree
\leq 1/\varepsilon
which is neither

\varepsilon
-additive nor
\varepsilon
-multiplicative,
and any finite set
A\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
with
|F|\geq|A|^{\varepsilon}
,
we have

|F(A)|=|\{f(a):f\in F,\;a\in A\}|\geq c|A|^{1+\eta}.

In this generality, we prove only existence of
\eta
without any bounds. This
is a limitation due to our rather indirect proof method.

However, Theorem
1.3
gives new information in the original
Elekes-Rónyai context of
F=\{f(x,b):b\in B\}
, in the “extremely
unbalanced” case that
B
is very small compared to
A
, and in this case we
do obtain bounds on
\eta
:

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
9.8
)
.

Suppose
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[x,y]
is neither additive nor multiplicative.
For all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C

such that for all finite
A,B\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
with
|A|\geq|B|\geq|A|^{\varepsilon}\geq C
,
we have
|f(A,B)|\geq|A|^{1+2^{-c\varepsilon^{-1}}}
,
where
c
is an absolute constant.

Previous such results
[
18
,
21
]
obtain this when
\varepsilon>\frac{1}{2}

(with a better exponent when
\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}\gg 0
).

In Theorem
9.8
, we moreover prove a multivariate version
of Theorem
1.4
:
for a polynomial
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[x,{\bar{y}}]
, we obtain
|f(A,B_{1},...,B_{n})|\geq|A|^{1+n^{-2}2^{-c\varepsilon^{-1}}}
for
|A|\geq|B_{i}|\geq|A|^{\varepsilon}\geq C
,
unless
f
is of the form
g(h(x)+k({\bar{y}}))
or
g(h(x)\cdot k({\bar{y}}))
.
This can be seen as an asymmetric version of the main result of
[
19
]
,
which with
|B_{i}|=|A|
obtains bounds of the form

\Omega(|A|^{1+\frac{1}{2}})
unless
f
is of the form
h(\sum_{i}g_{i}(x_{i}))
or

h(\prod_{i}g_{i}(x_{i}))
.

We also obtain a similarly unbalanced version of Elekes-Szabó:

Theorem 1.5

(Theorem
8.6
)
.

Suppose
S\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
is an irreducible complex algebraic surface which does
not project in any two co-ordinates to a curve,
and
S\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
is not in co-ordinatewise correspondence
with the graph of the group operation of a 1-dimensional algebraic group.
Then for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C>0
such that
for all finite subsets
A_{i}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
with

|A_{1}|=|A_{3}|
and
|A_{1}|^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\geq|A_{2}|\geq|A_{1}|^{\varepsilon}\geq C
,
we have

|S\cap(A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3})|\leq(|A_{1}||A_{2}|)^{1-\eta_{0}},

where

\eta_{0}=\varepsilon(1+\frac{1}{\varepsilon})^{-1}2^{-\frac{4}{c\varepsilon}+7}
and
c
is an
absolute constant.

We deduce the above results as consequences of our main theorem, which is a
similarly asymmetric and partially higher dimensional generalisation of the
Elekes-Szabó theorem.
Our approach to this is model-theoretic, continuing the line of investigation
developed in
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
,
[
1
]
,
[
2
]
,
and
[
3
]
. This involves finding, as a witness to the
asymptotically large intersections considered in Elekes-Szabó, a type in a
certain expansion of the field language by pseudofinite sets.
Our result is then most naturally formulated in terms of the properties of a
realisation of this type, as follows; the technical terms and notation
used here
will be defined in the body of the paper. An elementary finitary statement
appears as Theorem
8.4
.

Theorem 1.6

(Theorem
7.20
)
.

Suppose
a
,
d
, and
b
are broad tuples in an adequate expansion of the field
{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathcal{U}}
such that:

(i)

{\operatorname{tp}}(a)
is in coarse general position;

(ii)

{\operatorname{tp}}(d)
is in weak general position;

(iii)

a\sim_{d}b
(meaning
a
is field-theoretically interalgebraic
with
b
over
d
);

(iv)

d\sim{\operatorname{cb}}^{{\operatorname{ACF}}}(ab/d)
(i.e.
d
is (almost) canonical for the
interalgebraicity);

(v)

a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}d
and
d\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}b
.

Then there exists a connected commutative algebraic group
G
and a generic
pair
(g_{a},g_{d})\in G^{2}
, such that

a\sim g_{a}
and
d\sim g_{d}
and
b\sim g_{d}+g_{a}
.

A key idea in our proof is that the conditions (iii)-(v), with algebraic
independence in place of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
, are what we expect not just of a
triple
b=d+a
in a commutative algebraic group as in the conclusion, but
more generally of a generic triple
b=d*a
where
*
is a faithful
transitive action of an algebraic group on a variety. Our proof goes via
invoking the suitable version of the group configuration theorem to find such
a group action, then using the coarse general position assumption to show that
the group must actually be abelian, and the action therefore trivial.
When
{\operatorname{tp}}(d)
is in coarse general position (as in
Theorem
1.4
), we obtain the group configuration using
similar ideas to those applied in
[
1
]
to the original
Elekes-Szabó situation, but with a finite iteration required to get to the
group configuration – see Proposition
7.12
. More generally,
without this coarse general position assumption, we first obtain a family of
such groups by adding parameters to reduce to this case, then argue that this
family collapses to a single group.

As an application of some of the ideas in the proof, we obtain a group action
version Theorem
B.5
of the Balog-Szemerédi-Gowers theorem,
linking non-expansion conditions of the form
|S*A|\leq O(|A|^{1+\varepsilon})
to
approximate subgroups (where
*
is a faithful action of a group
G
on a set

X
and
S\subseteq G
and
A\subseteq X
are finite subsets). This is a
qualitative generalisation of the asymmetric Balog-Szemerédi-Gowers theorem

[
22
, Theorem 2.35]
; similar results were previously obtained in
[
15
]
.

1.1.
Structure of the paper

§§
2
-
4
develop the technical tools on pseudofinite dimensions we use in proving our main results:
§
2
recalls the basic definitions,
§
3
develops a version in our context of Balog-Szemerédi-Gowers,
and §
4
handles limits of types.
§
5
then proves that any faithful algebraic group action
(G,X)
containing asymptotically non-expanding finite subsets
(S,A)
must, if
A
is in suitably general position, be abelian.
§
6
explains how to recognise an algebraic group action from a generic algebraic configuration, specialising the group configuration theorem from model theory.
In §
7
we prove our main result Theorem
7.20
(introduced as Theorem
1.6
above); this goes via first proving a weaker version, Proposition
7.12
, which has a stronger general position assumption (this weaker version is already sufficient for the 1-dimensional asymmetric Elekes-Szabó and Elekes-Rónyai results mentioned above).
§
8
spells out the consequences of Theorem
7.20
for finite sets.
Next, §
9
proves our generalisation of Elekes-Rónyai, Theorem
9.4
(introduced as Theorem
1.3
above).
Appendix
A
repeats some of our earlier qualitative arguments in a quantitative way, perturbing our assumptions and tracing through the effect on our results. This is used to obtain the bounds on the exponents
\eta
in some of our results, as presented above.
Finally, Appendix
B
, which depends only on Sections
2
-
4
, proves our group action version Theorem
B.5
of Balog-Szemerédi-Gowers.

4.1
, Claim
6.5
, and the end of
Proposition
7.12
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notation

We use some abbreviations which are standard in model theory.
If
a
and
b
are tuples, then
ab
denotes the concatenated tuple
a^{\smallfrown}b
, while if
A
and
B
are sets,
AB
denotes
A\cup B
. We write
a\in A

to mean that each element of the tuple
a
is an element of the set
A
. All
tuples have finite length unless otherwise mentioned. Sometimes a tuple is
treated as the set of its elements.

Let
M
be an
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure.
If
C\subseteq M
, then
M^{{\mathcal{L}}(C)}
is the expansion of
M
to
{\mathcal{L}}(C):={\mathcal{L}}\cup C
, adding constants interpreted as the elements of
C
.
A
realisation
in
M
of a set of formulas
\Phi
in variables
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
is a (possibly infinite) sequence
{\bar{a}}=(a_{i})_{i\in I}
such that
M\vDash\phi({\bar{a}})
for each
\phi\in\Phi
. Such a set
\Phi
is

consistent
if every finite subset of
\Phi
has a realisation in
M
.
The
type
of a tuple
a
over a set
C\subseteq M
is the maximal set of

{\mathcal{L}}(C)
-formulas it realises,
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)={\operatorname{tp}}^{\mathcal{L}}(a/C)=\{\phi(x,c):M\vDash\phi(a,c);\;c\in C\}
.
We write
a\equiv_{C}b
to mean
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)={\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
.
When
C=\emptyset
, it is omitted.

A tuple
a
is
definable
over
C
if
a
is the unique realisation in
M
of some
{\mathcal{L}}(C)
-formula
\phi(x)
.
Let
{\operatorname{dcl}}(C)\subseteq M
be the set of those elements which are definable over
C
.
Tuples
a
and
b
are
interdefinable
over
C
if
{\operatorname{dcl}}(Ca)={\operatorname{dcl}}(Cb)
.

If
R
is a binary relation, such as
\equiv_{C}
or
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}
, we sometimes write
e.g.
aRbRc
as shorthand for “
aRb
and
bRc
”.

2.2.
Setup

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a countable first-order language.
Let
M=\prod_{i\to\mathcal{U}}M_{i}
be an ultraproduct of
{\mathcal{L}}
-structures
M_{i}

over some non-principal ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
{\mathbb{N}}
.

Fact 2.1

(
[
14
, Exercise 4.5.37, Lemma 4.3.17]
)
.

M
is
\aleph_{1}
-saturated: if
C\subseteq M
is a countable subset, then
any consistent set
\Phi((x_{i})_{i\in\aleph_{1}})
of
{\mathcal{L}}(C)
-formulas has a
realisation in
M
.

A subset
X\subseteq M^{n}
is
internal
if
X=\prod_{i\to\mathcal{U}}X_{i}
for some subsets
X_{i}\subseteq M_{i}^{n}
.

Let
\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathbb{N}}
.
Recall from
[
3
, §2.1, §2.2, Fact 2.2]

the following definitions of
\boldsymbol{\delta}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\xi}
on internal sets and
on tuples.
For
X
an internal set, we set
\boldsymbol{\delta}(X)=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\xi}(X):={\operatorname{st}}\log_{\xi}|X|\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
,
where
{\operatorname{st}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\mathcal{U}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
is
the standard part map.
In other words,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(\prod_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}}X_{i})
is (when finite) the metric
ultralimit in
{\mathbb{R}}
along
{\mathcal{U}}
of
\log_{\xi_{i}}(|X_{i}|)
, where
\xi=(\xi_{i})_{i\in\omega}/{\mathcal{U}}
.

For
C\subseteq M
countable and
a
a tuple from
M
,
let

\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathcal{L}}(a/C):=\inf_{\phi\in{\operatorname{tp}}^{\mathcal{L}}(a/C)}\boldsymbol{\delta}(\phi(M)).

We include
{\mathcal{L}}
in the notation only when we need to make it explicit.
More generally, for
p
a partial type we define
\boldsymbol{\delta}(p):=\inf_{\phi\in p}\boldsymbol{\delta}(\phi(M))
. By a
\bigwedge
-definable
set over
C
, we mean
a countable intersection
X=\bigcap X_{i}
of definable sets over
C
, and we
similarly define
\boldsymbol{\delta}(X)=\inf_{i}\boldsymbol{\delta}(X_{i})
; this is well-defined, see

[
1
, §2.1.5]
.

If
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C),\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)<\infty
,
we write
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
if
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)
,
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
otherwise.
More generally, if
A,B,C
are such that for all finite tuples
a\in A
and

b\in B
we have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C),\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)<\infty
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
, we write
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}B
.

We will always work in countable languages in which
\boldsymbol{\delta}
is continuous (meaning that
{\operatorname{tp}}(b)\mapsto\boldsymbol{\delta}(\phi(M,b))
is a well-defined continuous map for each
{\mathcal{L}}
-formula
\phi(x,y)
). Then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
is a well-behaved independence notion; we recall here
[
3
, Fact 2.2]
for reference.

Fact 2.2
.

Let
a,b,d
be tuples from
{\mathbb{K}}
and
C\subseteq{\mathbb{K}}
a countable subset,
and suppose
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C),\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C),\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)<\infty

•

{\operatorname{trd}}(a/C)=0\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=0
(Algebraicity)

•

a\equiv_{C}b\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)
(Invariance)

•

\boldsymbol{\delta}(ab/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)
(Additivity)

•

a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b\Leftrightarrow b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}a
(Symmetry)

•

ab\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}d\Leftrightarrow(b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}d\wedge a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{Cb}d)

(Monotonicity and Transitivity)

•

For any tuple
e
from
{\mathbb{K}}
, there exists
a^{\prime}\equiv_{C}a
such that
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}e
; (Extension)

more generally, any
\bigwedge
-definable set
X
over
C

contains an element
x
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(x/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(X)
.

Note that from additivity, it is easy to see that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cc)
for any
c
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(c/C)=0
. Thus,
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b\Rightarrow a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}bc
for any
c
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(c/Cb)=0
.

Note also that if
a\in{\operatorname{acl}}_{{\mathcal{L}}}(Cb)
, then
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)=0
,
and so by additivity
\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/Ca)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
,
and in particular
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)
.

A type
p
is
broad
if
0<\boldsymbol{\delta}(p)<\infty
; similarly for internal sets.

Definition 2.3
.

A
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independent sequence
in a type
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)<\infty

is a (possibly infinite) sequence
{\bar{a}}=(a_{i})_{i<\alpha}

of realisations of
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)

such that
a_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}a_{<i}
for all
i<\alpha
, where
a_{<i}=(a_{j})_{j<i}
.

The following fact and the lemma below are direct applications of
\aleph_{1}
-saturation of
M
.

Fact 2.4
.

[
2
, Section 1.1]

Let
p
be a partial type over a countable set of parameters. Then there is an internal set
Y\subseteq p(M)
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(Y)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(p)
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
f:X_{1}\times\cdots\times X_{n}\to Z
be a definable function and

p_{i}
a partial type in
X_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
defined over a
countable set
C
. Then

\boldsymbol{\delta}(f(p_{1}(M),\ldots,p_{n}(M)))=\max\{\boldsymbol{\delta}(f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})/C):a_{i}\in p_{i}(M),\text{ for
}1\leq i\leq m\}.

Proof.

Note that by
\aleph_{1}
-saturation,

f(p_{1}(M),\ldots,p_{n}(M))=\bigcap_{\phi_{i}\in p_{i}}f(\phi_{1}(M),\ldots,f_{n}(M))

is

\bigwedge
-definable, so the result follows from the “more generally” clause in Fact
2.2
(Extension).
∎

3.
Balog-Szemerédi-Gowers for types

Given subsets
A
and
B
of a group such that a large subset
Z
of
A\times B
has few distinct products
a\cdot b
for
(a,b)\in Z
,
the Balog–Szemerédi–Gowers (BSG) theorem
[
22
, Theorem 2.44]
extracts a reasonably large rectangle
A^{\prime}\times B^{\prime}\subseteq A\times B
with few distinct products. Further arguments of Tao,
[
23
, Theorem 5.4]
, go on to extract an approximate subgroup in this situation. In Proposition
3.2
, we give qualitative analogues of these results which work with complete types, passing from a non-expanding 2-type to a non-expanding product of 1-types over a
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independently extended base, and extracting a type for which arbitrarily long products still don’t expand.
For context, we then explain in §
3.1
how these can be seen as incarnations of the original results, by using them to to recover qualitative versions of those results.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
,
M
, and
\boldsymbol{\delta}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{\xi}
be as above, and assume that
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is continuous.
Let
(G,\cdot)
be an
{\mathcal{L}}
-definable group. We use
g\cdot h
to denote the product of
g
and
h
in
G
, while
gh
continues to denote the tuple
(g,h)
.

Definition 3.1
.

A set
D\subseteq M
is
locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite
if
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d)<\infty
for all tuples
d\in D
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
g,h\in G
be such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g),\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)<\infty
,
and

g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g.

Then there exists a countable locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite
D
with
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}gh

such that:

(i)

If
g^{\prime}\equiv_{D}g
and
h^{\prime}\equiv_{D}h
, then
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

(ii)

If
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
g_{i}\equiv_{D}g
for
i=1,\ldots,n
,
and if
g_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D}g_{i+1}
for odd
i\leq n
,
then
\boldsymbol{\delta}(\prod_{i\leq n}g_{i}^{(-1)^{i}}/D)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

Remark
.

In particular, (ii) applies to an arbitrary word in realisations of
{\operatorname{tp}}(g^{\prime-1}\cdot g/D)
, where
g^{\prime}\equiv_{D}g
and
g^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D}g
.

We delay the proof of Proposition
3.2
, and first obtain a consequence for group actions, which is what we will actually use in the main text (specifically, in Theorem
5.18
).

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
(G,X)
be an
{\mathcal{L}}
-definable group action.

Let
g,h\in G
and
a\in X
be such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g),\boldsymbol{\delta}(h),\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)<\infty
and

g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\text{ and }a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g*a.

Then there exists a countable locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite set
D
with
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}gha

such that
for any even-length alternating product
s=\prod_{1\leq i\leq 2n}g_{i}^{(-1)^{i}}
with
g_{i}\equiv_{D}g
for all
i\leq 2n
and with
g_{2m-1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D}g_{2m}
for all
m\leq n
,
and any
a^{\prime}\equiv_{D}a
,
we have

•

\boldsymbol{\delta}(s/D)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
;

•

\boldsymbol{\delta}(s*a^{\prime}/D)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)
.

Proof.

Since
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
, we can apply Proposition
3.2
. Let
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
be the locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite set given by the conclusion of Proposition
3.2
. Replacing
D
with a copy independent with
ha
over
g
, we may assume
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}gha
. Add
D
to the constants.

Let
s
,
g_{i}\equiv g
, and
a^{\prime}\equiv a
be as in the statement.
Then
\boldsymbol{\delta}(s)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
by Proposition
3.2
(ii); it remains to show
\boldsymbol{\delta}(s*a^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)
.

Let
g_{2n+1}a^{\prime}\equiv ga
be such that
g_{2n+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{a^{\prime}}s
.
Since
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}a
and hence
g_{2n+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}a^{\prime}
, we have
g_{2n+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}sa^{\prime}
.
Now
s^{\prime}:=s\cdot g_{2n+1}^{-1}=(\prod_{i\leq 2n+1}g_{i}^{(-1)^{i}})
, so
\boldsymbol{\delta}(s^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
by Proposition
3.2
(ii).
Also
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{2n+1}*a^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g*a)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)
.
So using
s*a^{\prime}=s^{\prime}*(g_{2n+1}*a^{\prime})
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\delta}(s*a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}(s^{\prime},g_{2n+1}*a^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{\delta}(s^{\prime},g_{2n+1}*a^{\prime}/s*a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(s^{\prime}/s,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{2n+1}/s,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a).\qed

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of
Proposition
3.2
. The definitions and results
introduced in the process will not be used in the rest of the paper.

We work in
M
: tuples are tuples from
M
, and sets of parameters are

countable
subsets of
M
; we use Fact
2.1
without mention. Let
E_{0}\subseteq M
be the set of interpretations of the constants of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Recall that a type
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/B)
is

•

finitely satisfiable in
C
if for any formula
\phi(x,b)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(a/B)
, there is
c\in C
such that
M\vDash\phi(c,b)
;

•

C
-invariant
if for any tuples
b,b^{\prime}\in B
with
b\equiv_{C}b^{\prime}
, we have
ab\equiv_{C}ab^{\prime}
.

We write
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}B
to mean that
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/CB)
is finitely satisfiable in

E_{0}C
.

Fact 3.4

(
[
20
, Example 2.17]
)
.

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}B
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/CB)
is
C
-invariant,
and for any
B^{\prime}
there is
a^{\prime}\equiv_{CB}a
such that
a^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}BB^{\prime}
.

We first show, in Lemma
3.7
, that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
can always be improved to
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}b
by working over additional
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}
-independent parameters, uniformly in
a
.

Definition 3.5
.

A type
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)<\infty
is
in coheir general position
(
chgp
) if
for any
a
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)<\infty
,

a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b\Rightarrow a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}b.

Lemma 3.6
.

(a)

If
b
and
b^{\prime}
are interdefinable over
C
, then

{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
is chgp if and only if
{\operatorname{tp}}(b^{\prime}/C)
is.

(b)

The converse implication
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b\Rightarrow a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}b

holds for any
a,b,C
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(ab/C)<\infty
.

Proof.

(a)

{\operatorname{tp}}(a/bC)
is finitely satisfiable in
E_{0}C
if
and only if
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/{\operatorname{dcl}}(bC))
is, and the result follows.

(b)

This follows from continuity of
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}_{C}b
but
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
, then
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}a
, so say

\boldsymbol{\delta}(\phi(x,a))<\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)=:\alpha
for some
{\mathcal{L}}(C)
-formula
\phi(x,a)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(b/aC)
,
hence by continuity there is some
\psi(y)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(\phi(x,d))<\alpha
whenever
\psi(d)
holds. Since
\phi(b,y)\land\psi(y)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
, by finite satisfiability already
\phi(b,c)\land\psi(c)
holds for some
c\in(E_{0}C)^{|a|}
, hence
\boldsymbol{\delta}(\phi(x,c))<\alpha
and
\phi(x,c)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
,
contradicting
\alpha=\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

Given
b
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(b)<\infty
, there exists a countable locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite
D
such that

D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}b
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/D)
is chgp.

Proof.

Recursively define countable sets
D_{i}
for
i\in\omega
as follows.
Let
D_{0}:=\emptyset
.
Given
D_{i}
with
D_{i}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}b
, enumerate as
(\phi^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
those
formulas
\phi(x)
over
bD_{i}
for which there exists
a
such that
\vDash\phi(a)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)<\infty
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D_{i}}b
.
If
\phi^{i}_{j}
and
a
are such, then let
d^{i}_{j}
realise a
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independent extension of
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/D_{i}b)
to
D_{i}b(d^{i}_{j^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}<j}
,
so that
\vDash\phi(d^{i}_{j})
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d^{i}_{j})<\infty
and
d^{i}_{j}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D_{i}}b(d^{i}_{j^{\prime}})_{j^{\prime}<j}
.
Let
D_{i+1}:=D_{i}\cup(d^{i}_{j})_{j\in\omega}
.
Then
D_{i+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}b
(by monotonicity and transitivity).

Let
D:=\bigcup_{i\in\omega}D_{i}
. Then
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}b
by finite character,
and
D
is countable and locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite by construction.
We conclude by verifying that
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/D)
is chgp.
So suppose
a
is such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/D)<\infty
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D}b
, and let
\phi(x)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(a/bD)
.
Then
\phi(x)\in{\operatorname{tp}}(a/bD_{i})
for some
i
,
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{D_{i}}b
since
b\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}Da
,
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)<\infty
because
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/d)<\infty
for some tuple
d\in D
,
so by construction there is
d\in D_{i+1}
such that
\phi(d)
, as required.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
g,h\in G
be such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g),\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)<\infty
,
and
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
,
and
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}h
.

Let
g^{\prime}\equiv g
and
h^{\prime}\equiv h
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

Proof.

We first find
g^{\prime\prime}
and
h^{\prime\prime}
such that

g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{\prime}h^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}\equiv g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}\equiv g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}\equiv gh

(This can be seen as an abstract form of the bound on “paths of length
three” in
[
22
, 6.20]
).

Let
h^{\prime\prime}
be such that
g^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}\equiv gh
and
h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{g^{\prime}}h^{\prime}
.
Then
h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{\prime}h^{\prime}
.
Let (by Fact
3.4
)
g^{\prime\prime}
be such that
g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}\equiv gh
and
g^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime}
.
Then
g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}\equiv g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime}
by invariance of finitely satisfiable types,
and
g^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h^{\prime}h^{\prime\prime}g^{\prime}
by Lemma
3.6
(
b
).
Then
g^{\prime\prime}h^{\prime\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{\prime}h^{\prime}
(by symmetry, monotonicity, and transitivity).

Now let
e:=(g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\cdot h^{\prime\prime},g^{\prime\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})
.
We have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h/h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g/h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
, and
similarly
\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(h/g\cdot h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g/g\cdot h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.
So
\boldsymbol{\delta}(e)\leq 3\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
,
and
g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}=(g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime\prime})\cdot(g^{\prime\prime}\cdot h^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\cdot(g^{\prime\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\in{\operatorname{dcl}}(e)
,
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(e/g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(e/g^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime}/g^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime\prime},h^{\prime\prime})=2\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

Hence
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(e)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(e/g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\leq 3\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)-2\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

By Lemma
3.7
, there exists a countable locally finite
D^{\prime}
such that
D^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(g/D^{\prime})
is chgp. Let
D\equiv_{g}D^{\prime}
be such that
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{g}h
. Then
{\operatorname{tp}}(g/D)
is chgp and
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}gh
by
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
and transitivity.
So we may expand the language by constants for
D
, since this does not affect the assumptions. Recalling our convention about constants in the definition of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}
, we then have that
{\operatorname{tp}}(g)
is chgp. We finish the proof by obtaining conclusions (i) and (ii) of the proposition with
D=\emptyset
, using that
{\operatorname{tp}}(g)
is chgp.

Since
{\operatorname{tp}}(g)
is chgp, we have
