g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}h
, so (i) follows immediately from Lemma
3.8
.

For (ii), we actually prove by induction on
n
the slightly more general statement that if
g_{0}=1
or
g_{0}\equiv g
, and
g_{i}\equiv g
for
i>0
, then
\boldsymbol{\delta}(\prod_{0\leq i\leq n}g_{i}^{(-1)^{i}})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

Claim 3.9
.

(a)

\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

(b)

\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{0}\cdot g_{1}^{-1})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

Proof.

Let
g^{\prime}h^{\prime}\equiv_{g\cdot h}gh
with
g^{\prime}h^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{g\cdot h}gh
.
Then
h^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h
, so
h^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}gh
, and then since
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h
we
have
hh^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
.
Similarly,
hh^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{\prime}
, and also
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{\prime}
.
Now
g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}=h\cdot h^{\prime-1}

since
g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime}=g\cdot h
,
hence
g^{-1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{-1}
.
By chgp we have
g^{-1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{u}g^{\prime}
,
so (a) follows from Lemma
3.8
.

Now note that
(g^{-1},g\cdot h)
also satisfies the conditions on
(g,h)
used in this argument,
namely that
g^{-1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h=g^{-1}\cdot g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g^{-1}

and
{\operatorname{tp}}(g^{-1})
is chgp (by Lemma
3.6
(
a
)).
So (b) holds by the same argument.
∎
3.9

By Claim
3.9
(b), we may assume
n\geq 2
.
Let
w:=\prod_{1\leq i\leq n-2}g_{i+2}^{(-1)^{i}}
,
so

b:=\prod_{0\leq i\leq n}g_{i}^{(-1)^{i}}=g_{0}\cdot g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2}\cdot w
.
By the inductive hypothesis for
n-2
, we have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{2}\cdot w)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.

Replacing
(g_{1},g_{2})
with another realisation of
{\operatorname{tp}}(g_{1},g_{2}/g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2})
(so
g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2}
and hence
b
don’t change),
we may assume
g_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2}}g_{0}b
.
But
g_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2}
,
since
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2}/g_{1})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{2}/g_{1})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{1}^{-1}\cdot g_{2})
(using
g_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g_{2}
and Claim
3.9
(a)).
Hence
g_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g_{0}b
. Then, using also Claim
3.9
(b),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\delta}(b)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{0}\cdot g_{1}^{-1},g_{2}\cdot w)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{0}\cdot g_{1}^{-1},g_{2}\cdot w/b)

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{0}\cdot g_{1}^{-1})+\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{2}\cdot w)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{0}\cdot g_{1}^{-1}/g_{0},b)

\displaystyle\leq 2\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{1}/g_{0},b)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g).\qed

3.1.
Recovering BSG

We now show how to deduce from Proposition
3.2

qualitative versions of the Balog–Szemerédi–Gowers theorem and Tao’s
corollary on the existence of approximate subgroups. In Appendix
B
,
we will generalise these results to a group action version. Neither this
subsection nor that appendix are used elsewhere in the text.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
G
be a definable group in
M
. We call an internal subset
X\subseteq G
a
coarse approximate subgroup
if
1\in X
,
X=X^{-1}:=\{g^{-1}:g\in X\}
and there is an internal subset
Z\subseteq G
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(Z)=0
such that
X\cdot X\subseteq Z\cdot X
.

Approximate subgroups have small doubling, namely the size of
X\cdot X
is roughly the same as
X
. Conversely, if a set has small tripling, it is not difficult to show one can obtain an approximate subgroup from it. The bounds involved are polynomial, which means we express this result in terms of
\boldsymbol{\delta}
as follows.

Fact 3.11
.

[
22
, Proposition 2.40]

Suppose
A
is an internal subset of
G
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(A\cdot A\cdot A)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)
. Let
H^{\prime}:=A\cup\{1\}\cup\{A^{-1}\}
and
H:=H^{\prime}\cdot H^{\prime}\cdot H^{\prime}
. Then
H
is a coarse approximate subgroup containing
A
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)
.

Corollary 3.12
.

(Qualitative Balog–Szemerédi–Gowers-Tao theorem)
Let
A,B\subseteq G
and
Z\subseteq A\times B
be internal subsets such that
0<\boldsymbol{\delta}(A),\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)<\infty
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(Z)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(A\cdot^{Z}B)\leq\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
, where
A\cdot^{Z}B:=\{a\cdot b:(a,b)\in Z\}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

(
[
22
, Theorem 2.44]
)
There are internal sets
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(A^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(B^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
, and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(A^{\prime}\cdot B^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
.

(2)

(
[
22
, Theorem 2.48]
)
There exist a coarse approximate subgroup
H
in
G
and
g,h\in G
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)\leq\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(A\cap(g\cdot H))\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(B\cap(H\cdot h))\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)
.

In fact, we also have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A^{\prime}\cdot B^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)
.

Remark 3.13
.

Item (2) implies item (1), but we will show how to obtain (1) directly from Proposition
3.2
(i).

We also remark here that item (2) provides a proof of
[
2
, Lemma 2.13]
which doesn’t depend on
[
23
]
, since weak general position (wgp, see Definition
5.4
) and Zariski density will be preserved in the coarse approximate subgroup
H
.

Proof.

Let
(g,h)\in Z
be such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g,h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(Z)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
(by Fact
2.2
(Extension)).
Since
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
, we get
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)
,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
, and
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h
.

Note that
g\cdot h\in A\cdot^{Z}B
, thus
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h)\leq\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)
. Therefore,

\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g,h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h,h)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)\leq\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)+\frac{3}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(h),

and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)
. Symmetrically,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.
Thus,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
. Since
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g,h)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(h/g\cdot h)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)+\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)-\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g),
we get
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
. Thus,
g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g
.

Now let
D
be the countable locally
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-finite set,
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independent with
gh
, given by
Proposition
3.2
. Add
D
to the constants.

We first show (1).
By Proposition
3.2
(i), for any
g^{\prime}\equiv g
and

h^{\prime}\equiv h
we have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime}\cdot h^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.
Let
s:={\operatorname{tp}}(g)
and
r:={\operatorname{tp}}(h)
.
By Lemma
2.5
,

\boldsymbol{\delta}(s(M)\cdot r(M))\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g).

Let
A^{\prime}\subseteq s(M)
be an internal subset with

\boldsymbol{\delta}(A^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(s)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq r(M)
with

\boldsymbol{\delta}(B^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(r)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(B)
(which exist by Fact
2.4
).
Then we get

\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(A^{\prime}\cdot B^{\prime})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(s(M)\cdot r(M))\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(A)+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\delta}(B).

Now we prove (2). Let
g^{\prime}\equiv g
with
g^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}gh
, and set
t:={\operatorname{tp}}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime})
. Then by Proposition
3.2
(ii),
\boldsymbol{\delta}(h_{0}\cdot h_{1}\cdot h_{2})\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
for any
h_{i}\models t
with
0\leq i\leq 2
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.5
,

\boldsymbol{\delta}(t(G)\cdot t(G)\cdot t(G))\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g).

Clearly, we also have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(t)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}/g^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
. Note that
{\operatorname{tp}}(g^{\prime-1}\cdot g/g^{\prime})\vDash x\in g^{\prime-1}\cdot A
. Let
h^{\prime}:=(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot h
. Then
B\cdot h^{\prime-1}\in{\operatorname{tp}}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}/h^{\prime})
. Since
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g,g^{\prime},h)=3\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime},h^{\prime},g^{\prime})
by
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independence,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(h^{\prime},g^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(h^{\prime}/g^{\prime})+\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g\cdot h/g^{\prime})+\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime})=2\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}/g^{\prime},h^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime},g^{\prime},h^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime},h^{\prime})\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
.
Let
t^{\prime}:={\operatorname{tp}}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}/g^{\prime},h^{\prime})
, and let
S\subseteq t^{\prime}(G)\subseteq t(G)
be an internal set with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(S)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(t^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
(which exists by Fact
2.4
), and set
H^{\prime}:=S\cup\{1\}\cup S^{-1}
and
H:=H^{\prime}\cdot H^{\prime}\cdot H^{\prime}
. Then
H
is a coarse approximate subgroup with
\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)

by Fact
3.11
.

Since
{\operatorname{tp}}(g^{\prime-1}\cdot g/g^{\prime})\vDash x\in g^{\prime-1}\cdot A
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(g^{-1}\cdot g^{\prime}/h^{\prime})\vDash x\in B\cdot h^{\prime-1}
, we have
A\supseteq g^{\prime}\cdot(t^{\prime}(G))^{-1}
and
B\supseteq t^{\prime}(G)\cdot h^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
(g^{\prime}\cdot H)\cap A\supseteq(g^{\prime}\cdot S^{-1})\cap A=g^{\prime}\cdot S^{-1}
and
(H\cdot h^{\prime})\cap B\supseteq(S\cdot h^{\prime})\cap B=S\cdot h^{\prime}
. We conclude:

\boldsymbol{\delta}((g^{\prime}\cdot H)\cap A)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(g^{\prime}\cdot S^{-1})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(H)\text{ and similarly }\boldsymbol{\delta}((H\cdot h^{\prime})\cap B)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}(H).\qed

4.
Adequate structures

This technical section exists to justify some limit constructions we use
later. In proving Theorem
5.18
, we will construct a sequence of
tuples which satisfy
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
-independence relations up to an error which tends
to zero, and Lemma
4.1
(
c
) will allow us to
extract a “limit” tuple which satisfies the independence relations
precisely. This involves expanding the language, and the work of this section
is to provide a setup in which this can be done harmlessly.

Let
M=\prod_{i\to\mathcal{U}}M_{i}
be an ultraproduct of sets
M_{i}
over some
non-principal ultrafilter
\mathcal{U}
on
{\mathbb{N}}
. If
{\mathcal{L}}
is a first-order
language, we write
M^{\mathcal{L}}
to denote an
{\mathcal{L}}
-structure on
M
. Call a structure

M^{\mathcal{L}}

internal
if every
{\mathcal{L}}
-definable set is internal; equivalently,

M^{\mathcal{L}}=\prod_{i\to\mathcal{U}}M_{i}^{\mathcal{L}}
for some
{\mathcal{L}}
-structures
M_{i}^{\mathcal{L}}
on

M_{i}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists a class of internal structures on
M
in countable languages,
which we call
adequate
structures,
such that:

•

If
M^{{\mathcal{L}}_{0}}
is an internal structure in a countable language

{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
, then it admits an adequate expansion
M^{{\mathcal{L}}}
for some
{\mathcal{L}}\supseteq{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
.

•

If
M^{\mathcal{L}}
is an adequate structure and
A_{0}
is an infinite

{\mathcal{L}}
-definable set, then the following hold with
\boldsymbol{\delta}:=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{|A_{0}|}
:

(a)

\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is continuous.

(b)

If
M^{\mathcal{L}}
is adequate and
C\subseteq M
is countable, then the
expansion by constants
M^{{\mathcal{L}}(C)}
is adequate.

(c)

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
let
a_{i}\in M^{n}
for
i\in\omega
,
and let
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
be an ultrafilter on
\omega
.

Then there exist
a\in M^{n}
, a countable language
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\supseteq{\mathcal{L}}
, and
an adequate expansion
M^{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
of
M^{{\mathcal{L}}}
, such that for any

{\mathcal{L}}
-definable function
h:M^{n}\to M^{<\omega}
:

(i)

{\operatorname{tp}}^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)=\lim_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\operatorname{tp}}^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a_{i})
,

(ii)

\boldsymbol{\delta}^{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}(h(a))=\lim_{i\rightarrow{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathcal{L}}(h(a_{i}))
,

(iii)

Suppose
(Z_{b})_{b\in B}
is an
{\mathcal{L}}
-definable family,
and for
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
-many
i
, we have
\boldsymbol{\delta}({\operatorname{tp}}^{\mathcal{L}}(h(a_{i}))\cup\{x\in Z_{b}\})\leq\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
for all
b\in B
.
Then
\boldsymbol{\delta}({\operatorname{tp}}^{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}(h(a))\cup\{x\in Z_{b}\})\leq\alpha
for all
b\in B
.

(iv)

The constants of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
are just the constants of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

To indicate the purpose of this notion of adequacy, consider trying to obtain (
c
)(ii) without passing to an expansion. If
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a_{i})\leq\alpha
for all
i
, then we should also have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a)\leq\alpha
. But the internal sets
X_{i}\ni a_{i}
witnessing
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a_{i})\leq\alpha
might vary with
i
, so simply taking
a
to realise
\lim_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\operatorname{tp}}^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a_{i})
might not be enough. The proof will go via adding limits of such
X_{i}
to the language so as to obtain the upper bound. Note that no one countable expansion will suffice for all such limit constructions.

We avoid adding new constant symbols in (
c
) because
constants will play a special role in our formalism – see
Assumption
5.1
and the definition of
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}
.

Proof.

Given a set
M_{0}
, we define the following first-order structure in a
countable language
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
The sorts are:
M_{0}
,
and for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
a sort
P_{m}:=\mathcal{P}(M_{0}^{m})
for the set of all
subsets of
M_{0}^{m}
. The language consists of
(m+1)
-ary membership
relations
\in_{m}
on
M_{0}^{m}\times P_{m}
for each
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We use
\in
to
refer to any of the
\in_{m}
, treated as a binary relation in the natural
way.

Write
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
for the resulting structure obtained from
M_{i}
. Consider the ultraproduct
{\mathcal{S}}:=\prod_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
.

Let
{\operatorname{Int}}:=\bigcup_{m}\prod_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}}\mathcal{P}(M_{i}^{m})=\bigcup_{m}P_{m}({\mathcal{S}})
, the
set of internal subsets of powers of
M
. Consider
{\operatorname{Int}}
with the structure
induced from
{\mathcal{S}}
, i.e. such that the definable sets are those defined by

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}
-formulas with no free variable of sort
M
. (In fact
{\operatorname{Int}}
is
bi-definable with
{\mathcal{S}}
, interpreting
M
by the singleton sets in
P_{1}
,
but we will not use this.)

Say
L\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
is
adequate
if
L
is a countable elementary
substructure of
{\operatorname{Int}}
.

We define an internal structure
M^{\mathcal{L}}
to be
adequate
if
{\mathcal{L}}
is
countable and the set of
{\mathcal{L}}
-definable sets is adequate.

We associate to an adequate
L\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
an adequate structure

M^{{\mathcal{L}}_{L}}
, with a relation for each
R\in L
interpreted tautologically,
i.e.
M^{{\mathcal{L}}_{L}}\vDash R(a)\Leftrightarrow{\mathcal{S}}\vDash a\in R
.
Since boolean combinations and existential quantification can be expressed
through definable functions in
{\operatorname{Int}}
, every
{\mathcal{L}}_{L}
-definable set is indeed
defined by a basic relation
R\in L
.

We conclude by verifying the properties in the statement.

Let
M^{{\mathcal{L}}_{0}}
be an internal structure in a countable language; we show
that it has an adequate expansion.
Let
L_{0}\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
be the set of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
-definable sets.
By Löwenheim-Skolem there exists an adequate
L
with
L_{0}\subseteq L
, and
then
M^{{\mathcal{L}}_{L}}
is as required.

Now suppose
M^{\mathcal{L}}
is adequate, and let
L
be the (adequate) set of all

{\mathcal{L}}
-definable sets. Fix a pseudofinite set
A_{0}\in L
and set
\boldsymbol{\delta}:=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{A_{0}}
.
First observe:

Claim 4.3
.

Given
q\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{\geq 0}
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there is an

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(A_{0})
-formula
\theta_{q}(Z)
with free variable of sort
P_{m}
such that
{\operatorname{Int}}\vDash\theta(Z)\Leftrightarrow|Z|\leq|A_{0}|^{q}
.

Proof.

If
q=\frac{k}{n}
, we may take a formula expressing the existence
of an injective function
Z^{n}\rightarrow A_{0}^{k}

(as an element of
P_{nm+km^{\prime}}
, where
A_{0}\in P_{m^{\prime}}
).
∎
4.3

We verify the claims (a)-(c).

(a)

If
R\in L
, then by Claim
4.3
so is the internal set

\{y:\exists^{\leq|A_{0}|^{q}}x\;(x,y)\in R\}=\{y:\theta_{q}(R_{y})\}
,
since this is the value at
R
of an
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(A_{0})
-definable function.
So
{\mathcal{L}}
is closed under such cardinality quantifiers,
and hence
\boldsymbol{\delta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is continuous (as in
[
1
, §2.1.6]
).

(b)

Let
L^{\prime}\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
be the set of
{\mathcal{L}}(C)
-definable sets.
We show
L^{\prime}\preceq{\operatorname{Int}}
.
So let
Z\subseteq P_{n}
be a non-empty
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(L^{\prime})
-definable set; we
aim to show
Z\cap L^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.
We can write the parameters from
L^{\prime}
used to define
Z
as

R_{1}({\bar{c}}),...,R_{k}({\bar{c}})
where
{\bar{c}}\in C^{m}
and
R_{i}\in L
.
So
Z=Z^{\prime}({\bar{c}})
where
Z^{\prime}\subseteq P_{n}\times M^{m}
is
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(L)

definable.
Let
\pi(Z^{\prime})\subseteq M^{m}
be the projection.
Then
Y:=\{X\in P_{n+m}:\forall{\bar{x}}\in\pi(Z^{\prime})\;X({\bar{x}})\in Z^{\prime}({\bar{x}})\}\subseteq P_{n+m}
is also
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(L)
definable and non-empty,
and
L\preceq{\operatorname{Int}}
,
so say
X_{0}\in Y\cap L
.
Then
X_{0}({\bar{c}})\in Z\cap L^{\prime}
, as required.

(c)

Let
a_{i}\in M^{n}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
be as in the statement.

Given
a\in M^{n}
and
L\subseteq L^{\prime}\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
, write
(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)

for the structure with universe
L^{\prime}
and basic predicates for the trace on

L^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\vec{L},a)
-formulas with all free variables of sorts from

{\operatorname{Int}}
, where
\vec{L}
denotes a tuple enumerating
L
.

Claim 4.4
.

There exists a countable
L^{\prime}\preceq{\operatorname{Int}}
with
L\subseteq L^{\prime}

and
a\in M^{n}

such that
(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)\vDash\lim_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\operatorname{Th}}(L,\vec{L},a_{i})
.

Proof.

By Löwenheim-Skolem, say
{\mathcal{N}}
is a countable model of this theory.
By
\aleph_{1}
-saturation of
{\mathcal{S}}
, let
a\in M^{n}
realise
\lim_{i\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}{\operatorname{tp}}^{{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}}(a_{i}/\vec{L})
.
Then by
\aleph_{1}
-saturation again, the diagram of
{\mathcal{N}}
is realised by
some
L^{\prime}\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
such that
{\mathcal{N}}\cong(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)
, since
consistency of finite parts of the diagram is ensured by existential
statements in
{\operatorname{tp}}^{{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}}(a/\vec{L})
.
Finally,
L^{\prime}\preceq{\operatorname{Int}}
since in particular
L^{\prime}\vDash{\operatorname{Th}}^{{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}}(L)={\operatorname{Th}}({\operatorname{Int}})
, and
L^{\prime}\subseteq{\operatorname{Int}}
is a substructure
in a language with predicates for
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}
-formulas.
∎

Consider the corresponding adequate expansion
M^{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}
of
M^{\mathcal{L}}
.
Now condition (iv) from the statement holds by construction, and we conclude
by observing that the language of
(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)
is expressive enough for
(i)-(iii) to follow from Claim
4.4
:

(i)

If
X\in L
, then
a\in X
is expressed by a sentence in the language of

(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)
.
1
1

1

This is immediate if when defining
(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)
we
include 0-ary relation symbols interpreted as
{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{S}}(\vec{L},a)
-sentences;
if instead we use a logical formalism which does not include 0-ary
relation symbols, we can make do with sentences of the form
\exists Y(Y=Y\land a\in X)
.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{\delta}^{{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}}(h(a))\leq\alpha
is expressed in the
language of
(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)
via Claim
4.3
by
\{\exists R\in L^{\prime}\;(h(a)\in R\land\theta_{q}(R)):q\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{>\alpha}\}
.

(iii)

Let
q>\alpha
.
By
\aleph_{1}
-saturation of
{\mathcal{S}}
,
for
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
-many
i
we have
\exists R\in L\;(h(a_{i})\in R\land\forall b\in B\;\theta_{q}(R\cap Z_{b}))
;
indeed, otherwise we obtain (for this
i
) finite consistency of the countable

{\mathcal{S}}
-type in
y

\{\neg\theta_{q}(R^{\prime}\cap Z_{y}):R^{\prime}\in L,\;h(a_{i})\in R^{\prime}\}\cup\{y\in B\},

and a realisation
b\in B
contradicts the assumption, since
q>\alpha
.
So the corresponding statement holds for
(L^{\prime},\vec{L},a)
, and we conclude
since
q>\alpha
was arbitrary.
∎

5.
Non-expansion in homogeneous spaces

In this section, we prove as Theorem
5.18
a variant of

[
3
, Theorem A.4]
on nilpotence of “non-expanding” homogeneous
spaces, strengthening the general position assumption on the space (from weak
to coarse general position) and strengthening the conclusion to abelianity.

5.1.
Further setup and preliminaries

From this section on, we work in a non-principal ultrapower
K:={\mathbb{C}}^{{\mathcal{U}}}
of
the complex numbers.
We consider
K
as an adequate expansion of the field structure in a language

{\mathcal{L}}\supseteq{\mathcal{L}}_{\operatorname{ring}}
,
with
\boldsymbol{\delta}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{|A_{0}|}
for some infinite
{\mathcal{L}}
-definable set
A_{0}
. We also
assume:

Assumption 5.1
.

The set
E_{0}
of interpretations of the constants in
{\mathcal{L}}
is an algebraically closed subfield of
K
.

Unless otherwise specified, all sets of parameters are
countable

subsets of
K
, and all tuples are finite tuples from
K
.

As in
[
3
, Definition 2.4]
, we use superscript-0 to refer to
the reduct of
K
to the ring language expanded by the constants of
{\mathcal{L}}
. In particular,
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C)
is the smallest algebraically closed subfield of
K
containing
CE_{0}
, and
\dim^{0}(a/C)
denotes the transcendence degree of
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Ca)
over
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C)
, while
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}b
means
\dim^{0}(a/Cb)=\dim^{0}(a/C)
, and
{\operatorname{loc}}^{0}(a/C)
is the smallest algebraic subvariety over
CE_{0}
containing
a
.
We write
a\sim^{0}_{C}b
to mean
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(aC)={\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(bC)
.

Our emphasis on expansions of the ring language by constants is motivated by the following situation. Suppose a pseudofinite set
X
, which is Zariski dense in an algebraic group
G
, is nevertheless concentrated on a coset
cH
of an algebraic subgroup
H\leq G
, in the sense that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(X\cap cH)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(X)
. In this case, it is important to isolate the relevant coset. To do so, we expand the language by naming the element
c
as a constant and work with the set
X\cap cH
instead of
X
. This reduction will be implemented by passing to types in weak general position after adding constants (see Definition
5.4
and Lemma
5.8
).

If
V
is an algebraic variety over a subfield
k\leq K
,
by a
generic
element over
k
of
V
we
mean an element
a\in V(K)
not in any lower-dimensional subvariety of
V
defined
over
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(k)
. By a
generic element of
V
we mean a generic element
over some field
k
over which
V
is defined. Unless otherwise stated, by
a
subvariety
of a variety
V
, we mean a closed subvariety.

We will use a little of the model theoretic terminology of canonical bases.
See
[
1
, §2.1.12]
for a relevant exposition; briefly, we use

{\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(a/C)
to refer to the
{\operatorname{ACF}}
-canonical base of
{\operatorname{tp}}^{0}(a/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C))
. That is, the field generated over the constants of
{\mathcal{L}}
by
{\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(a/C)
coincides with the field
generated over the constants by the field of definition of
{\operatorname{loc}}^{0}(a/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C))
.
We often use this in the context of the following fact, whose proof is a straightforward exercise.

Fact 5.2
.

Suppose
a\sim^{0}_{d}b
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}d\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}b
.
Then
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}({\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(ab/d))
is the smallest
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}
-closed subset of

{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(d)
over which
a
and
b
are
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}
-interalgebraic:

{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}({\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(ab/d))=\bigcap\{{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(d^{\prime}):d^{\prime}\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(d),\;a\sim^{0}_{d^{\prime}}b\}.

We also use the following standard result.

Fact 5.3

(
[
16
, Lemma 1.2.28]
)
.

If
C={\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C)
, then
{\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(a/C)\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}((a_{i})_{i\in\omega})
(and even
{\operatorname{dcl}}^{0}((a_{i})_{i<\omega})
) whenever
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
is a

Morley sequence
in
{\operatorname{tp}}^{0}(a/C)
,
meaning
a_{i}\equiv^{0}_{C}a
and
a_{i+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}a_{0}\ldots a_{i}
.

5.2.
General position

We use the following definitions of general position:

Definition 5.4
.

For a (countable) set
C\subseteq K
and a tuple
a\in K
,

•

{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is
in weak general position
(
wgp
) if
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)<\infty
and for any tuple
b\in K
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}b
, we have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
.

•

{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is
in coarse general position
(
cgp
) if
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)<\infty
and for any tuple
b\in K
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}b
, we have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)=0
.

Remark 5.5
.

It follows immediately from these definitions that any cgp type is also wgp.
To ensure this holds, our definition of cgp is slightly different from that in
[
1
, Definition 5.6]
, but the definitions agree when
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)>0
.

Remark 5.6
.

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is wgp and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=0
, then no
b
as in the definition exists, so
a\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C)
.

Remark 5.7
.

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is broad and
\dim^{0}(a/C)=1
, for example if
a
is an element of
K
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is cgp.
Indeed, if
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}b
then
a\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb)
, so
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)=0<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
.

Part (i) of the following lemma, which is essentially a reformulation of

[
12
, Lemma 2.13]
, shows that weak general position is a harmless
assumption from our coarse perspective, in the sense that we can always
ensure it by working over parameters which do not affect
\boldsymbol{\delta}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
be a broad type.

(i)

There exists
d
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)=0
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cd)
is wgp, in particular
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cd)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
.

(ii)

There exists
d
such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)<\infty
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cd)
is cgp and broad.

Proof.

(i)

Let
b\in K
be a finite tuple such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
and
\dim(a/Cb)
achieves the minimal value for such
b
.
Then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is wgp;
indeed, if
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{Cb}b^{\prime}
for some tuple
b^{\prime}\in K
, then by the minimality of
\dim(a/Cb)
, we must have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cbb^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)
.
Let
d:={\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(a/Cb)
.
Let
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
be a
\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independent sequence of realisations of
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb))
.
Since
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is wgp,
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
is a Morley sequence in
{\operatorname{tp}}^{0}(a/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb))
.
Then by Fact
5.3
,
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb)\cap{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}({\bar{a}})
where

{\bar{a}}
is a finite subsequence of
(a_{i})_{i\in\omega}
.
But then
{\bar{a}}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
and hence
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)=0
; we can see this explicitly as follows.
Note that

\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/C)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb))=|{\bar{a}}|\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb))=|{\bar{a}}|\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)\geq\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/C).

Hence,
\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/Cd)=\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/C)
(by
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb)
) and
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}d/C)-\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/Cd)=\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/C)-\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/Cd)=0
, where
\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}d/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/C)
by
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}({\bar{a}})
.

(ii)

Let
b\in K
be a finite tuple such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)>0
and
\dim(a/Cb)
achieves the minimal value for such
b
. Such a
b
exists since
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)>0
.
Then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is cgp;
indeed, if
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{Cb}b^{\prime}
for some tuple
b^{\prime}\in K
, then by the minimality of
\dim(a/Cb)
, we must have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cbb^{\prime})=0
.
Let
d:={\operatorname{cb}}^{0}(a/Cb)
.
Then by the same argument as before,
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}({\bar{a}})
, where
{\bar{a}}
is a

\boldsymbol{\delta}
-independent sequence of realisations of
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(Cb))
. Hence,

\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)<\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}}/C)\leq|{\bar{a}}|\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)<\infty
.

∎

Fact 5.9

(
[
3
, Lemma 2.13]
)
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C),\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)<\infty
.

(i)

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is wgp and
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(aC)
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(d/C)
is wgp.

(ii)

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
are wgp, then so is
{\operatorname{tp}}(ab/C)
.

(iii)

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is wgp, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is wgp.

Remark 5.10
.

From (ii) and (iii), it is immediate that if
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
and both
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
are wgp, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(ab/C)
is wgp (however, this does not hold for cgp).

Remark 5.11
.

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is wgp and
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(aC)\setminus{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C)
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(d/C)
is broad;
this can be seen from (i) and Remark
5.6
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C),\boldsymbol{\delta}(b/C)<\infty
.

(i)

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is cgp and
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(aC)
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(d/C)
is cgp.

(ii)

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is cgp, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is
cgp.

(iii)

If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
and
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
is wgp and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is
cgp, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is cgp.

Proof.

(i)

If
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(C)
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(d/C)
is clearly cgp.
Otherwise, by Remark
5.11
,
{\operatorname{tp}}(d/C)
is broad.
Suppose
d\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}e
for some
e
.
Then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}e
(as
d\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(aC))
.
Thus,
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Ce)=0
by
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
being cgp,
and so
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/Ce)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Ce)=0<\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)
.

(ii)

Suppose
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is cgp and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{Cb}d
for some
d
.
Then
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}bd
, and hence

\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cbd)=0<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb),

by
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
being cgp and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
.
Therefore,
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is cgp.

(iii)

Suppose
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}d
for some
d
.
Replacing
d
with a canonical base as in the proof of Lemma
5.8
,
we may assume
\boldsymbol{\delta}(d/C)<\infty
.
Choose
d^{\prime}\equiv_{Ca}d
such that

d^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{Ca}b
(such a
d^{\prime}
exists by extension).
Since
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
, by transitivity we obtain
d^{\prime}a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{C}b
, and hence

a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{Cd^{\prime}}b
.

Now
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}b
since
{\operatorname{tp}}(b/C)
is wgp,
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}d^{\prime}
since
ad\equiv_{C}ad^{\prime}
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{C}d
,
so
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 8.00134pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 3.92064pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchar 12854\relax$\kern 2.00034pt\hss}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{0}_{Cb}d^{\prime}
.
Thus,
0=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cbd^{\prime})<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cb)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
,
by the assumption that
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/Cb)
is cgp.
Using
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{Cd^{\prime}}b
, we conclude that
0=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cd)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cd^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/Cd^{\prime}b)<\boldsymbol{\delta}(a/C)
.
Hence
{\operatorname{tp}}(a/C)
is cgp, as desired.

∎

Lemma 5.13
.

Suppose
{\bar{a}}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k})
is a finite sequence such that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a_{i})=\boldsymbol{\delta}(a_{j})<\infty
and
\dim^{0}(a_{i})=\dim^{0}(a_{j})
for all
i,j
, and
a_{i+1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}a_{1}\ldots a_{i}
for all
i
.
Let
b\in{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}({\bar{a}})
. Then:

(i)

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a_{i})
is wgp for all
i
, then
{\operatorname{tp}}(b)
is wgp.

(ii)

If
{\operatorname{tp}}(a_{i})
is cgp for all
i
, then

\boldsymbol{\delta}({\bar{a}})\cdot\dim^{0}(b)\leq\boldsymbol{\delta}(b)\cdot\dim^{0}({\bar{a}})
.

Proof.

(i)

This follows by induction on
k
from Fact
5.9
.

(ii)

This is immediate if
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a_{i})=0
. Otherwise, rescaling, we may assume
\boldsymbol{\delta}(a_{i})=\dim^{0}(a_{i})
.
The statement is then an instance of
[
1
, Lemma 5.13]
.
∎

5.3.
Algebraic homogeneous spaces

Definition 5.14
.

An
algebraic homogeneous space
is a pair
(G,X)
, where
G
is an algebraic group and
X
an algebraic variety, along with a transitive action of
G
on
X
such that the
corresponding map
*:G\times X\rightarrow X
is a regular map.
We say
(G,X)
is a
faithful algebraic homogeneous space
if in addition
the action is faithful,
and we say
(G,X)
is
connected
if
G
is connected.
We abbreviate “connected faithful algebraic homogeneous space” to
CFAHS
.
We write
g\cdot h
for the group operation on
G
, and
g*x
for the action.
We say
(G,X)
is
defined over
a field
C
if the algebraic group

G
, the variety
X
, and the action
*
are all defined in the algebraic
sense over
C
(i.e. by
{\mathcal{L}}_{\operatorname{ring}}(C)
-formulas).

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
(G,X)
be a faithful transitive (abstract) group action, let
a\in X
,
and let
G_{a}
be the stabiliser of
a
in
G
. Then
G_{a}\cap Z(G)=\{1\}
.

Proof.

Let
g\in G_{a}\cap Z(G)
. By transitivity of the action,
for any
b\in X
, there is
h\in G
with
b=h*a
. Then

g*b=g\cdot h*a=h\cdot g*a=h*a=b
. Hence,
g=1
by faithfulness.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
(G,X)
be a CFAHS. Suppose
G
is nilpotent but non-abelian.
Then no orbit of the centre is generic in
X
,
i.e.
\dim(Z(G)*a)<\dim(X)
for all
a\in X
.

Proof.

Let
a\in X
.
By Lemma
5.15
,
G_{a}\cap Z(G)=\{1\}
, and
Z(G)
acts
regularly on
Z(G)*a
, and so
\dim(Z(G))=\dim(Z(G)*a)
.

Considering orbits and stabilisers, we have

\dim(G)=\dim(G*a)+\dim(G_{a})=\dim(X)+\dim(G_{a})
.
Suppose towards a contradiction that
\dim(Z(G)*a)=\dim(X)
,
so
\dim(G)=\dim(Z(G))+\dim(G_{a})
.
Consider the map
\eta:Z(G)\times G_{a}\to G
sending
(g,h)\mapsto g\cdot h
. It
is a group homomorphism with trivial kernel, so its image has dimension

\dim(Z(G))+\dim(G_{a})=\dim(G)
. Thus,
\eta
is a group isomorphism by
connectedness of
G
. Now
G_{a}
is non-trivial since
G\neq Z(G)
, and
G_{a}

is nilpotent since
G
is, so
Z(G_{a})
is non-trivial. However, we must have

Z(G_{a})\leq Z(G)
, as
G\cong Z(G)\times G_{a}
. This contradicts the fact
that
G_{a}\cap Z(G)
is trivial.
∎

Recall that the
nilpotency class
of a nilpotent group
G
is the least
k
such that

[g_{0},\ldots,g_{k}]:=[\ldots[[g_{0},g_{1}],g_{2}],\ldots g_{k}]=1
for all
g_{0},\ldots,g_{k}\in G
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
G
be a connected nilpotent algebraic group over a
field
K_{0}
, and say
G
has nilpotency class
k
.
Let
(g_{0},\ldots,g_{k-1})\in G^{k}
be generic over
K_{0}
,
and let
t:=[g_{0},\ldots,g_{k-1}]
.

Then
t
is central in
G
, and
{\operatorname{trd}}(t/K_{0})>0
.

Proof.

First,
t
is central as
[g_{0},\ldots,g_{k-1},g_{k}]=1
for any
g_{k}\in G
.
Suppose for a contradiction that
t\in{\operatorname{acl}}(K_{0})
. Then
[h_{0},\ldots,h_{k-1}]=t
for all
h_{i}\in G
by the genericity (since
G
is connected and
\{(x_{0},\cdots,x_{k-1}):[x_{0},\ldots,x_{k-1}]=t\}
is a Zariski-closed set in
G^{k}
), so
t=1
and we contradict
G
having nilpotency class
k
.
∎

5.4.
Abelianity

Theorem 5.18
.

Let
(G,X)
be a CFAHS defined over
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(\emptyset)
.

Let
g\in G
be generic, let
a\in X
be generic,
and suppose
{\operatorname{tp}}(g)
is wgp and
{\operatorname{tp}}(a)
is cgp,
and
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g*a
.

Then
G
is abelian, and so
(G,X)
is isomorphic over
{\operatorname{acl}}^{0}(\emptyset)

with the homogeneous space
(G,G)
in which
G
acts on itself by addition.

Let us summarise the proof strategy before going into the details of the proof. Consider first the special case that the group action is
G
acting on itself, and suppose moreover that in addition to
h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g\cdot h
, we have
g\cdot h\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\boldsymbol{\delta}}h
, or equivalently
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)
. Then we obtain a (coarse) approximate subgroup by Balog-Szemerédi-Gowers, and it was observed in
[
2
, Proposition 2.21]
(and implicitly already in
[
5
]
) that
{\operatorname{tp}}(h)
being cgp then forces
G
to be abelian.
To get from this special case to the generality of our statement, there are two obstacles. First, we may have a non-trivial group action. If the stabilisers are trivial, we are essentially in the above case of the group acting on itself. Since we work with algebraic group actions, we can easily reduce to the case of generically trivial stabilisers by considering the diagonal action of
G
on
X^{n}
for sufficiently large
n
. (In fact our arguments also go through for arbitrary group actions with suitable conditions on the stabilisers, and we formulate a result of this form in Appendix
B
.) Secondly, we may not be in the symmetric situation that
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)=\boldsymbol{\delta}(h)
. We handle this by iterating, essentially as in
[
3
, Theorem A.4]
. Replacing
g
by
g_{1}:=g^{\prime-1}\cdot g
where
g^{\prime}
is an independent copy of
g
over
g\cdot h
, we obtain a new triple
(g_{1},h,g_{1}\cdot h)
. We may have
\boldsymbol{\delta}(g_{1})>\boldsymbol{\delta}(g)
, but
